12.5.1 Historical notes and institutional tasks 12.5.2 Research, transfer and definitive arrangement of the Italian Fallen Legislative References APPENDIX: Possible evolutionary lines 10


The large majorities registered in Parliament in support of the missions in Albania and Kosovo, in the past legislature, and in support of our participation in "Enduring Freedom" demonstrate that Italy has developed full national awareness of intemational responsibilities, regardless of contingents majority and opposition roles.
The issues of military security, geopolitics and geoeconomics have become, even in Italy, topics of collective interest, assuming, in the media, a relevance that was not imaginable until the recent past. The White Paper also intends to represent this new awareness of the problems.
The 2002 White Paper was bom from the Govemment's desire to take stock of the situation of the Armed Forces and, more generally, of the entire Defense sector based on the new geo-political framework that emerged after the mass murders perpetrated on 11 September against The United States of America.
Italy and the Italians have closely followed our military in the many operations outside national borders from Africa to the Balkans to Timor; they appreciated his work, his dedication to duty and his professionalism. At the same time, public opinion was an active spectator of the evolution of the military instrument, in particular the entry of women and the launch of the professional model: real and incisive changes in national customs and traditions.
Sixteen years separate us from the previous White Paper, created in 1985 by the then Minister Spadolini.
The White Paper not only serves to wam the country about the centrality of Defence, broadening and consolidating attention and consensus around the Armed Forces and their needs, but also to photograph the existing situation in order to evaluate the effects of the process of change on the national military instrument, compared to the military instruments of other countries in the world, within the framework of an Atlantic Alliance that is rethinking itself and of a Europe aiming to equip itself with a political structure also in the military field.
The Atlantic Alliance, after confirming its role in the management of the crises in the Balkans, has become a mechanism also characterized by "inclusive" logics, no longer just one of opposition to the threat. The openings to the Russian Federation, following Moscow's decisive stance against intemational terrorism, certainly mark the end of an era and give the Alliance greater flexibility, serving both the political role and the military function, within the framework of common values that unite the most recently democratic countries with those that have already consolidated it.
In those years the East-West confrontation was alive, with the issue of Euromissiles, but new commitments were already appearing, anticipated by the missions in Lebanon from 1982 to 1984, as if to announce that season of radical changes in the intemational scenarios that would then open in 1989, with the fall of the Berlin Wall.
The end of the Cold War and the disintegration of the USSR caused ethnic, nationalist, religious and economic tensions to explode along the "arc of crises", as strategic experts call the vast area of the planet that starts, in the West, from North Africa, crosses the Middle East and the Caucasus, extends eastwards to the Gulf, Central Asia, Kashmir until reaching South-East Asia where conflicts of an ethnic-religious nature occur (just think of the case of East Timor). To the North this "arc of crises" has the Balkan appendix, contiguous to our land and maritime borders, through which a complex conflict, with ancient historical roots, has wedged itself into the very heart of Europe.
The progress of the EU's common security and defense policy is another of the comerstones of the security of Italy and - more generally - of Europe and the entire planet. Italy participates in the efforts aimed at creating a first, broad nucleus of European forces, as decided in Helsinki; we know that there are many difficulties as demonstrated by the long, ten-year joumey towards the single currency. However, it is necessary to continue on the path of the common security and defense policy, in parallel with the reform of the Union's institutions, now unavoidable especially in light of the forthcoming enlargement of the Union to the East and towards the Baltic and Mediterranean areas.
The September 11 attacks definitively marked the new reality of security Even the national Armed Forces have changed profoundly compared to the configuration they had in correspondence with the previous White Paper, in 1985. The reform of the top management is a reality with all its beneficial consequences in terms of unity of command and joint vision of the entire military instrument ; the Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it planetary and have confirmed that the threats to be faced in the future have a very different nature from those we have fought and defeated in the past, thanks to the Atlantic cohesion which has united the European and North American democracies in a common effort to defend freedom. Today, an identical cohesion - possibly with a wider participation of States - must counter the dark threat of terrorism , which could end up having weapons of mass destruction and demonstrates the capacity for global organisation, penetration and proselytism not only in areas affected by crises but even in Westem societies. ____________ At the same time, the other innovation that marks a radical break with the context in which the previous White Paper developed is the different approach of public opinion towards the issue of national security.
PREMISE 11


This reformulation of the structure will naturally also have to be extended to the complex of top management bodies which add up - despite the reforms implemented - to 5 General Staff, 10 General Directorates and 2 Central Offices in addition to the Intermediate Commands, Inspectorates and various bodies.
Carabinieri have become the fourth Armed Force; the technical-operational area is still undergoing simplification and streamlining like the technical-administrative and technical-industrial areas. The objective of quality is pursued with greater determination even if many expectations of modemization and professionalization have been frustrated by the limited resources available: a true "sword of Damocles" for national defense and of which only now - under the pressure of terrible threat of intemational terrorism – the negative effect on national security is clearly and widely perceived.
Secondly, another sector to consider is that of means and materials where it appears necessary to define priorities more carefully. On the other hand, the sector is no longer conceivable as an expression of national productivity, but as the result of intemational collaboration and cooperation both due to the high costs achieved by the new weapon systems and the need for so-called "interoperability" within the framework of our alliances .
In concrete terms, the legacy that the 2nd Berlusconi govemment received essentially consists in the large participation in peace-keeping operations, carried out admirably by our military, which however, in terms of presence, altemation and tum-over, is reduced to 9,000 men multiplied by 3: that is, 27,000 soldiers or, at most, 30,000. A small number compared to the 112,000 men of the Army and the approximately 200,000 soldiers of the three "traditional" Armed Forces (excluding the Carabinieri, equal to another 116,000 units), at the end of the "professionalization" phase.
The Air Force and the Navy are currently the Armed Forces most suffering; especially the Air Force, which is not only assessable in terms of aircraft, but also in terms of electronic control of airspace. The recent terrorist attack on the United States has dramatically highlighted the pressing need to ensure security from attacks from the air. Unfortunately, the Italian flaws in this sector are very substantial and emphasized by the lack of piloted interceptors, missiles and radars. Therefore the control of the airspace, which is part of the first task of the Armed Forces in the framework of the defense of the territory, as stated, in its most complete sense of defense of the State, in article 1 of law no. 331 on the suspension of compulsory conscription, will constitute one of the urgent priorities for the near future.
The Navy is not experiencing better times, also because the prolonged use of ships in naval blockade tasks and interventions implemented in the last decade has wom out the ships beyond measure, shortening their average lifespan calculated on the basis of the employment rates of the war era cold.
The numerous series of legislative measures passed by the Govemments of the past 13th legislature, despite the appreciable intention of modemizing the defensive instrument, can be seen as a shock dose inoculated into a body that is not in perfect health because it has been undemourished or cared for a long time with the bare minimum. However, the sudden and massive cure does not yet seem capable of dealing with operational needs which today approach the maximum possibilities sustainable by our military instrument and, moreover, in a transition phase from compulsory conscription to voluntary service and with increasingly pressing intemational obligations due to developments in the terrorist activity of Islamic fundamentalism.
Finally, attention must inevitably focus on the Defense budget, which cannot be separated from the Defense model.
The 2002 Defense budget set by the previous Govemment amounts to 36,837 billion, equal to 1.48% of GDP. But the rate allocated to the "defense function" is 26,460 billion, equal to 1.06% of GDP. If we compare these data with the homogeneous data of other NATO countries, we find negative differences. In fact, France allocates 1.75% to the defense function, England 2.48%, Germany 1.18% but compared to a GDP 70% higher than that of Italy.
It is true that today Armies are no longer evaluated in quantitative terms, much less as sums of men. But the relationship between the Operational Departments and the General Staff is symptomatic, from which we can derive certain imbalances in presence, predominant among the Top Commands, Intermediate Commands and School Bodies, compared to the "operational" ones. Nor does this disharmony diminish when moving on to the Navy and Air Force where the function of the machine prevails over man, yet the intermediate Command structures are not perfectly sized for the complex of Ships and Flight Units.
Imbalances in the use of personnel, overall undercapitalization of the forces, qualitative level of the military instrument lower than the average of our main allies: these appear to be the main handicaps of our Defense which can weigh, and not a little, also on the role and authority of the 'Italy in intemational forums.
We need answers.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it Firstly, the whole staffing sector needs a balancing act and this will be the ____________ first objective of the new Govemment which has also set itself the goal of reducing the transition phase from "conscription" to military "professionalism" so as to be able to conclude by 2004, before the end of the legislature, the formation of fully professionalized Armed Forces in the number of 190,000 men.
12


The book was edited by General Pietro Giannattasio, Head of the Office for Military Policy (office of direct collaboration with the Minister of Defense), who was its inspiration. The structure was modeled on the one adopted in the 1985 volume. The text is based on the material provided by the General Staff for the drafting of the individual parts and is aimed at photographing the existing one on 18 October 2001, therefore it does not indicate the developments subsequent ones.
General Pietro Giannattasio added, upon my directive, his personal assessment of the possible evolutionary lines of defense policy. The essay, contained in the appendix, represents a precious contribution provided by an authoritative expert who simply indicates the topics that may, in the future, constitute the subject of in-depth analysis with the aim of increasing, without prejudice to the organic volumes established by law, the efficiency of the operational units. The White Book will be updated every year with a file containing all the changes that have occurred in the meantime. The Govemment's and my personal gratitude for their efforts goes to General Giannattasio and to those who made this meritorious work possible.
NOTE However, it is undeniable that we are faced with an “unbalanced budget” and the ideal division of It is true that, compared to the 2001 budget, there was an increase of 8.2%, but it is also true that in the distribution of expenses for personnel, for operations and for investment there was an increase for personnel far superior (+11.6%) to those of operations (+3.0%) and investments (+8.0%). The first of those events, twelve years later, can already be framed in a minimum of historical perspective. The second announces a current situation with consequences of extraordinary evolutionary importance.

PART I 14


But the protection of national security today takes on a broader meaning which includes, in addition to the defense of national sovereignty, the contribution to intemational stability and security, the legitimate safeguarding and protection of our interests as well as the prevention of old and new risks, and the fight against violations of law and peace.
The "missions" of our Armed Forces today are defined within a vast spectrum of operational actions aimed, in compliance with constitutional principles, at the protection of national security, political-territorial integrity, the values of our civilization and well-being and economic and social development.
At the same time, an extraordinary adaptation effort by national and intemational defense organizations has involved the revision of strategic concepts and doctrines, to deal with new threats that have become multifaceted, widespread and multidirectional, and such as to require multidimensional and multinational responses.
The current phase of the Euro-Atlantic security framework is heir to the transformations that occurred within NATO and the EU in the last decade of the last century, a process in which Italy played an important role, contributing to overcoming the old barriers that divided Europe and the updating of the transatlantic link, also combining in a qualified manner the strategic objectives of European integration with those of the consolidation of the Euro-Atlantic spaces.
This new geostrategic phase places European and allied military instruments faced with new operational needs and the need to acquire the flexibility of use necessary to face new missions with continuously variable geometry.
Compared to the old threat, the current risks, but also the challenges and opportunities, are more complex in nature and require broader and more diversified responses. Intemational action, in addition to the traditional political, diplomatic, economic, cultural and cooperation instruments, increasingly makes active use of the military instrument, which has become one of the essential indicators of the credibility and reliability of the country system in the context of intemational relations.
This is how, in the last decade of the last millennium, the Atlantic Alliance was affected by a profound process of reforms. The United States of America have reviewed their strategic priorities, their operational tools, their intemational commitments. The European Union is committed to developing its own common foreign, security and defense policy.
The Mediterranean, with its strong economic imbalances between north and south, its multiple conflicts and tensions, its centrality as a route of economic, civil and military communication, has taken on an increasingly high strategic value. Eastem countries are looking for a place in Europe and in the world, with a strong anchoring in the West and its value system.
The inaction of the European scenario during the Cold War was replaced by a picture of instability and new security risks which pushed Italy, together with its European and Atlantic allies, to substantially rethink and redesign its military policy action .
But the risks transcend national borders. In an increasingly interconnected environment, the "amount of risk" accepted by a nation included in intemational coalitions represents the risk shared by all. ____________ The evolution from that threat to these risks represents a real difference between what was and is today a condition of systemic crisis, which negatively influences the pursuit of the country's vital interests.
Today, security and defense policies find themselves no longer able to limit themselves to the traditional sphere of strictly war capabilities, but also have to deal with a transversal, often subtle and sometimes immaterial enemy.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it The new configuration of the global framework therefore requires a continuous and careful re-evaluation, first political and then strategic, of defense priorities also aimed at setting up new military capabilities and new force systems, capable of facing a diversified multiplicity of new risks.
Consequently, support for the intemational community's operational missions has become, particularly in recent years, a characterizing element in the use of our Armed Forces.
It is a multiplicity of asymmetric activities which, variously combined and articulated together, configure those risks that the Intemational Community will have to deal with in the future. Terrorism represents a catalyst and multiplier factor for these risks.
The traditional threat no longer exists, real risks arise which base their capabilities not only on armaments, but also on more diversified and sophisticated tools, such as the movement of money or legitimate and illicit commercial goods, which allow large profits, which can be used for criminal purposes.
Naturally, this concept is increasingly embedded in the action of the large intemational organizations of which we are an active and responsible part, in particular the European Union, the Atlantic Alliance and the United Nations.
The terrorist attacks of 11 September 2001 further upset previous geostrategic structures, projecting new risks onto the intemational scene deriving from possible terrorist actions and organized crime.

Thanks to this significant participation in intemational initiatives, as well as a strong proactive capacity, Italy has acquired a growing global political "weight" and, from a "consumer" country of security, has become a "producer" country of security.
1.2 The global framework and the subjects of intemational politics: UN, WEU, EU, NATO, OSCE This principle of pre-eminence of the UN mandate derives from its universalism which gives it the necessary legitimacy and political authority.
The UN is the first intemational institution of reference for its pre-eminent role in the prevention and political management of crises and in globally addressing new challenges and the increased risk of instability in the various regions of the world.
Italy is characterized, in the intemational context, by a strong presence in Intemational Security and Defense Organizations.
The lines of the national and intemational Security and Defense Policy have demonstrated, in fact, a significant sharing of choices of a "bipartisan" nature and, therefore, more authoritative and credible in the intemational context. synergy of contributions from individual member countries. At the same time, intemational institutions have proven to be only partially capable of managing new crises and conflicts.
This commitment has developed in a context in which the country has recently demonstrated its ability to clearly identify its security interests, strengthening its national identity.

1.2.3 The North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO) But, in the face of this undisputed institutional and moral pre-eminence, the UN does not appear to have a sufficiently effective structure for the direct management of complex crises that involve the use of military force.
1.2.1 General information In this difficult decade animated by armed conflicts, wars and intemational crises that have tragically affected all continents, including Europe, new principles have slowly been established in the Intemational Community in ways that were unthinkable in the old system, even if still insufficient to define a new system of relations between states.
16


Two new tasks have instead been inserted: that of crisis management, to be carried out "case by case and by consensus of individual nations", and that of cooperation with other countries in the Euro-Atlantic area, (partnership) .
The most significant aspect of the New Strategic Concept consisted in the reworking of the tasks (fundamental security tasks): with the confirmation of those relating to security, consultation and deterrence and defense, but with the elimination of that of maintaining strategic balances in Europe, considered no longer current.
1.2.3.1 The New Strategic Concept of NATO The evolutionary process that led to the definition of the New Strategic Concept of NATO, most recently finalized in the Washington Summit (1999), began in the early 1990s with the "Declaration of London", which started a project to adapt the organization's structures to the new security context resulting from the disappearance of the East-West confrontation.
This brings us to Washington (1999), where, essentially, NATO redefines itself as an Organization for the 21st century. of new crisis management and peacekeeping missions.
The transatlantic bond thus remains the foundation of Westem security, in a context of accelerated transformation towards a more balanced and equitable relationship of responsibilities and duties between Europeans and the United States.
The concept of security had to be understood in an even more global way than in the past, including risks of a different nature such as the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction and their means, the interruption of flows of vital resources, acts of terrorism and sabotage.
The "New Strategic Concept", recognizing the security factor as intimately connected to political, economic and social considerations, based the existence of a collective defense capacity on dialogue and cooperation, to be achieved through collaboration with the new partners of the Central and Eastem Europe.
From a defense organization against the threat from the East, therefore "exclusive", the Alliance has transformed, with a series of intemal and extemal reforms, into a builder of Euro-Atlantic security, capable of projecting, to the East and the South, stability and safety, therefore "inclusive".
Following the definition of the New Strategic Concept, the guidelines were drawn up for: • the review of the political and military structures and procedures of the Alliance; • the reduction of Alliance forces and their response levels, with a restructuring in operation The North Atlantic Treaty Organization (NATO), the bastion of our national defense during the Cold War, remains the comerstone of our security even today, but in the new forms towards which the Atlantic Alliance is evolving, both in terms of missions and intemal arrangements.
At present, this cooperative form, which makes use of the participation of 27 partner countries, in addition to those of the Alliance, can rightly be considered a fundamental component of security in the Euro-Atlantic area, a truly central moment of NATO policy. •
• reaffirms the willingness to intervene with its own procedures, while respecting the autonomous ones ____________ ability to work together in peacekeeping, crisis management and generally humanitarian tasks.

At the Brussels summit in January 1994, the Partnership for Peace (PfP) initiative was inaugurated, in its dual function of cooperation instrument, but also of preparation and screening of candidate countries for the eastward enlargement of the Alliance.
The subsequent Rome Summit (1991) created the foundations for a more institutional system of relations with the countries of Central and Eastem Europe, with the establishment of a forum, the North Atlantic Cooperation Council (NACC), which initially involved 9 nations.
Furthermore, the Alliance, in working to achieve its purpose and fulfill its fundamental security tasks: • recognizes the "authority of the United Nations Security Council and the responsibility of the OSCE for the On this occasion, in fact, the "new" NATO saw the light of day.
A third fundamental element was added to the dialogue-deterrence combination, namely cooperation with former adversaries, potential future partners, in search of a new type of relationship that would guarantee the highest possible level of peace and stability in the Eurozone. Atlantic.

It is intended to strengthen the "European pillar" of the Alliance through a clear acceleration of the process of modemizing operational capabilities.

Finally, the actual implementation of the new structures will follow (4th and final phase).

Both of these steps require European countries to face a process of renewal and adaptation which requires them to assimilate and develop a safety culture that is essential for managing new scenarios.
The inclusion of the Petersberg missions in the Treaty of Amsterdam created the conditions for the decision of the 1999 Cologne European Summit to bring elements of the WEU into the European Union. At the same time, the evolution of the transatlantic relationship has favored the emergence of the European Security and Defense Identity (ESDI).
Italy participates in OSCE operations by sending numerous experts and with a financial contribution to long-term missions, exceeding 10% of the total.
The CFE Treaty, the Code of Conduct, the Vienna Document 1999, the charter for European Security, the management of disarmament agreements in the former Yugoslavia, the study of new confidence and security measures (CSBMs) are some of the tools concrete measures with which the OSCE develops its activity.
In 1997, the European Union, which in the meantime had established itself in the economic dimension and strengthened in the political one, began to establish itself as a new actor in the security of the continent also thanks to the launch of the Euro-Mediterranean Partnership (Barcelona, 1995).
Such progress has paved the way for the development of the EU in the field of Defense and Security, according to a criterion of hereditary continuation between the two institutions.
For twenty years, the OSCE has played an important role in preventive diplomacy and in the in-depth resolution of conflict causes, operating mainly in sectors relating to civil coexistence, such as the control of the democracy of electoral and political-institutional, humanitarian and social processes, the prevention of conflicts or the re-establishment of normal post-conflict conditions.
Even if the operational role of the organization was not considered "decisive", the WEU can be credited with achieving important goals such as the creation of common structures in the analysis of satellite intelligence, the development of a European security concept, the management of the first operations with intemational police forces and the creation of "European multinational forces".
CFE (Conventional Forces in Europe), signed in 1990 by the countries belonging to NATO and those of the Warsaw Pact, which represents the comerstone of conventional disarmament on the continent. ____________ This entailed a redefinition of the WEU, envisaging its development "as a defense component of the European Union and as an instrument to strengthen the European pillar of the Atlantic Alliance". Ultimately, for the WEU, it was configured as a real role as a “hinge” between NATO and the EU.
The creation of the European Union in November 1993 and the entry into force of the Maastricht Treaty subsequently led to the development of the "Common Foreign and Security Policy" (CFSP). It has been defined as the “defence of common values, fundamental interests and the independence of the Union; in maintaining peace and strengthening intemational security according to the principles of the United Nations Charter; in the development and consolidation of democracy and the rule of law and in the definition of a common defense policy, which could subsequently lead to a common defense".
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it European Defense fits into both the context of the Atlantic Alliance and that of the European Union. For the first area, the reference framework is constituted by the decisions taken at the Atlantic Council in Berlin (3 June 1996) where support for the development of a European Security and Defense Identity was agreed This objective requires the availability of adequate operational capabilities to support policies to expand the framework of security, democracy and stability beyond the current borders of the Union. An expansion, which is fully within the interests of a highly industrialized country like Italy, for which the freedom and security of commercial and economic exchanges constitute a vital interest.
The definition of the WEU's role had a significant evolution in June 1992, when the so-called "Petersberg Declaration" was drawn up, which entrusted it with the use of the Armed Forces of European Nations for tasks, known as "Petersberg missions", including humanitarian, peacekeeping and crisis management activities.
Europe's evolutionary process in the security and defense sector has primarily involved the Westem European Union, the oldest post-war European defense institution.

• the launch of a civilian dimension of crisis management, through the creation of a rapid police intervention force of 5000 men, of which 1000 can be deployed within 30 days (in this regard The primary objective, "Headline Goal", formulated at the Helsinki summit, necessary to develop European

Furthermore, the principles of consultation with NATO on military issues were defined and the following four priority areas towards which the EU will develop its relations with NATO were identified: • security issues; • capacity objectives;

At the European Council of S. Maria di Feira (19-20 June 2000) it was decided to organize a capabilities commitment conference, with the intention of moving from a political objective to a precise military assessment of needs, on the basis of which each State can determine their participation in the collective effort and from which the Fifteen will together identify the unsatisfied needs and the relative means to satisfy them.
The subsequent European Council in Lisbon (22 May 2000) established a Committee with specific competence for the civil aspects of crisis management and for the related strengthening and use of non-military instruments.
Consistent with these determinations, Italy - which attributes particular importance to the EU initiative - has offered a qualified conceptual contribution in the various sectors of intervention (police, civil protection, humanitarian aid, activities for the reconstruction of institutions, electoral observers, administrative experts, etc.).

These capabilities are the subject of bilateral or multilateral partnerships within the EU and which go to complete the land, naval and air capabilities in which the Fifteen have committed to participate.

In Nice, a concept for the use of European police forces was developed, including both civil and military police forces, according to two scenarios: the first, which aims to strengthen local police capacities with training missions, assistance, control and consultancy; the second - which corresponds to complex crises - involves the use of EU police forces to replace local police forces to carry out missions aimed at re-establishing public security. ____________ The definitive structure of the new institutional bodies created to manage this policy provides for an important role for the Political Security Committee (PSC). It will be essential that this Committee has the capacity to become a steering body for crisis management and to establish effective interactions with the North Atlantic Council, thus ensuring a reliable and transparent relationship between the Union and the Alliance.
In particular, the start was made for the establishment of permanent bodies responsible for carrying out, under the impetus of the High Representative for Foreign and Security Policy, a more active and responsible role in the field of security and crisis management . They are: the permanent Political and Security Committee (PSC) , based in Brussels, composed of national representatives at senior official/ambassador level. It is the forum for dealing with all matters relating to the CFSP. In the case of military crisis management operations, the CPS will have to ensure, under the authority of the Council, the political control and strategic direction of the operations; the Military Committee (CM), composed of the Chiefs of Defense Staff, represented by their military delegates. It is the forum for providing military advice and recommendations to the CPS, as well as ensuring the military direction of the General Staff. The President of the CM will participate in Council meetings when decisions with Defense implications must be made; the General Staff (MS), operating within the Council, to provide military advice and support to the CFSP, including the execution of military crisis management operations under EU leadership. The General Staff will ensure early waming, situation assessment and strategic planning within the Petersberg missions, including the identification of national and multinational European forces to EU membership and European countries that are part of NATO but not the EU).
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it The first of the main components of the process of building a Common European Security and Defense Policy is the institutional management.
With reference to the particular implications inherent in decisions relating to the use of military force, the institutional direction for Europe will have to be able to combine the unity of the overall framework of the Union with the specificity of security and defense policy compared to other policies community.
• the definition of permanent consultation methods between the European Union and NATO; • the integration of the WEU's own functions into the EU; • agreements on the methods of consultation and participation of potential partners (candidate countries There is a very high degree of political sharing, on the part of everyone, of the project for the creation of effective European military capabilities, linked and coherent with those of NATO, even of those countries traditionally considered more prudent by nature. With reference to the initial objective set in Helsinki, a "Catalogue of Capabilities" has been prepared necessary to deal with all types of crisis management missions, from those of a strictly humanitarian type, to the more demanding maintenance or imposition of peace.
In defining these capabilities - both quantitatively and qualitatively - the document outlines a coherent connection with the Defense Capabilities Initiative (DCI), decided by the Alliance at the Washington summit in 1999.
1.2.6.2 Future developments of European Defense The Union, in implementing a European Security and Defense Policy, faces a great integration challenge, equal in complexity and scope to that of the euro. Just as the euro is fully inserted into the global economy, so the European Security and Defense policy must be firmly interconnected with the security policy of the Atlantic Alliance: two sides of the same coin, distinct but not separate, under penalty of loss of effectiveness and of value of our own security and therefore of the process of European political integration. the Carabinieri contributes approximately 800 men, placing it first among all European nations);
The second component of the process concems the reform of military instruments. For them there is a need for an organizational and doctrinal transformation and in the use of men and means. A transformation that requires a leap in quality consistent with Helsinki's ambitious yet realistic objective, i.e. the achievement of an adequate operational capacity by 2003.
23


The sectors in which to invest with greater determination have been identified as interoperability, in the availability of forces that can be projected on a professional basis, in strategic mobility, in Command and Control, in Communication, in Surveillance and Intelligence, in precision engagement, in self-protection and the ability to operate in risky environments due to the phenomenon of the proliferation of weapons of mass destruction.
Among these, the incidence of military spending compared to GDP, the ratio between investment expenditure and operating expenditure; the percentage of spending dedicated to research and development; levels of military spending per capita; the quantity of professional forces available compared to the size of the population.
These are all indicators for which today there is a strong lack of homogeneity among Europeans, even one to five, and for which convergence paths should be outlined that would facilitate the contribution of each European country to achieving the common objective of an increased European military capacity . Adopting convergence criteria for military spending, albeit in a flexible and not rigidly prescriptive way, as was the case with the single currency, would greatly facilitate the process of creating an effective European Security and Defense Policy.
The convergence between the operational objectives outlined in the European Defense Initiative and in the "Defense Capability Initiative" of the Atlantic Alliance ensures the integration of one into the other, according to the concept of "separable but not separate forces".
In this sense, the convergence of European defense policies is a necessity to meet the modemization objectives both within NATO and Europe. In this context, after having defined, as a premise, a homogeneous picture of current European military spending, it could be appropriate and effective to indicate lines of progressive approach by European countries to some qualitative standards of military spending, identifying some indicators that outline objectives of convergence.
These shortcomings have repercussions both on the process of building European Defense and on the European capabilities available to the Alliance. In filling these shortcomings - as is necessary - each national contribution will therefore take on a double value: on the European side and on the Atlantic side, thus outlining a connection between the two areas, that of the Union and that of the Alliance.
This "gap", between the required capabilities, defined in the Helsinki Catalogue, and those that Europeans are able to make available to the Union today, constitutes the challenge to make the Union's extemal action more credible, providing it with effective decision-making and operational capabilities.
European spending must certainly be optimized in all sectors, but it must also be increased in investments and advanced technologies. The convergence process must direct us towards reducing the common factor costs present in each of our military instruments, the so-called "overheads", by concentrating and rationalizing the management structures in support of the operational forces, and progressively integrating military demand, both in terms of operational requirements and acquisition processes.
However, in defining national contributions to the common European objective, the shortcomings of operational capabilities of European countries have emerged.
The simple reductive restructuring, although necessary and ongoing, of the quantity of forces of European countries, is not sufficient to modemize European military instruments to reduce the operational gap mentioned above.
The military gap between Europe and the United States is such that it reflects on the real ability to decide and operate together; in essence, the split in military capabilities risks tuming into an operational and political split between the United States and Europe, which puts European security at risk. ____________ They represent only 10 – 15% of US capabilities. It is easy to see how the cost of the lack of European convergence is equal to 50% of the missing capabilities. To this assessment we can add the estimate made by NATO military authorities, according to which the cost of the operational gap between the United States and EU countries would amount to around
30 billion dollars. These estimates, although questionable in absolute terms, are indicative of a real, "political" as well as operational phenomenon.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it European countries as a whole allocate around 60% of US military spending to defence, while the Europeans' operational and projection capabilities are far from having a value proportional to it.
1.3 Innovative process of National Defense 1.3.1 Political-Military Relations The construction of European Defense cannot ignore the problem of adequate resources. The effectiveness of military spending represents the third component of the process of building the European Security and Defense Policy.
It is from this perspective that the discussion and appropriate decision-making processes on the concept of convergence criteria for European Defense must be promoted in European institutional settings.
24


The network of intemational relations in which Defense participates is completed by the activity of qualified support to the Ministry of Foreign Affairs in the different negotiating forums for disarmament and non-proliferation (UN, OSCE, EU, NATO, Geneva Conference on Disarmament) and by active and direct implementation of the Treaties and Conventions ratified by Italy (CIVA).
The Vienna Document, signed by the 54 OSCE countries (55 with the readmission of the Federal Republic of Yugoslavia), introduces confidence and security measures including the exchange of information, notification of unusual military activities, inspection and observation visits, military contacts. Its first draft was in 1990. It was followed by three other versions, the last of which was in 1999.
The Dayton Agreements provided for stabilization measures in the former Yugoslav area in general and Bosnian in particular. In Annex IB, the two articles, II and IV, of a predominantly military nature, tended to reconcile the various Bosnian components and to rebuild trust between the states involved (Croatia, Serbia and the Entity of Bosnia-Herzegovina). Article V, of an eminently political nature, completed the stabilization process involving not only all the countries in the area but, on a voluntary basis, also extemal nations.
Generality.
1.3.2. Disarmament and non-proliferation regimes The ratification of the adopted Agreement, currently carried out only by Belarus and Ukraine, was subordinated by all Westem countries to full compliance by Moscow with the limits set by the current treaty, exceeded as a result of the Chechen crisis, and with the commitments to withdraw from Georgia and Moldova.
The link between foreign policy and defense policy has consequently become very close.
Of note are the bilateral agreements on the withdrawal of Russian forces from Georgia and Moldova, the full implementation of which, costly in terms of financial and technological resources, requires the support of Westem nations.
In the new intemational framework, exposed to profound geo-strategic transformations, defense policies have become a factor of primary importance, increasingly called upon to make their contribution and in any case to be crucial for the definition of new intemational relations.
As a corollary to the text of the agreement, various nations have signed political commitments of various kinds. ____________ The CFE Treaty, signed in 1990 by NATO and Warsaw Pact countries, establishes the maximum quantity of armaments of five categories (tanks, armored vehicles, artillery, fighter planes and attack helicopters) that each group of States Parties can possess.
On Nov. 19. 1999, at the OSCE Summit in ISTANBUL, the CFE States Parties signed a new text of the convention adapted to the new geo-strategic reality, eliminating the structure of opposing blocs and establishing specific limits for each State Party.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it This is due to the fact that, despite the positive evolution of the strategic context and the high improbability of a large-scale conventional aggression directed against the Alliance, the possibility of the emergence of such a long-term threat persists.
Lowering levels of armaments and verification measures resulting from arms control and non-proliferation agreements support NATO's political and military efforts to achieve strategic objectives. Among these, the strengthening of stability on the European continent, made possible by the CFE Treaty, the substantial reductions in nuclear weapons made possible by the START treaties, the signing of the Comprehensive Test Ban Treaty (CTBT), the automatic and unconditional renewal of the Treaty on the Non-Proliferation of Nuclear Weapons (NPT), the entry into force of the Chemical Weapons Convention (CWC), the aforementioned Ottawa Convention on the Ban of Anti-Personnel Mines and other Confidence-Security Measures (CSBMs) developed by 'OSCEs which, despite being only politically binding, contribute to détente in crisis areas.
The Stability Pact for South-Eastem Europe, signed by the Ministers of Foreign Affairs of numerous States, including Italy, and representatives of various intemational organisations, pursues the aim of defining the strategy of interventions in Europe of the South-East, create synergies between initiatives launched by anyone and in any context and ensure added political value to the actions undertaken. It operates through a regional discussion table and three thematic
tables relating, respectively, to high Democratization and Human Rights, to Economic Reconstruction, Development and Cooperation and to Security. In the latter, Defense directly follows projects relating to arms control, non-proliferation, military contacts and control of small arms. Two officers expert in arms control constitute the national contribution to a Regional Arms Control Verification and Implementation Assistance Center (RACVIAC), established in Zagreb.

In order to contain costs and optimize available resources, ten nations, including Italy, have joined forces to develop a common observation system called "Pod System". This system consists of a removable extemal container containing the sensors and some consoles to be mounted on board the C130 H aircraft.
While awaiting the entry into force of the Treaty, Italy, like other countries, conducted a series of test flights (bilateral and multilateral) with both Westem and Eastem European countries.
The convention on the Prohibition of Chemical Weapons, ratified by the Italian Parliament with Law No. 496 of 18
November 1995 and which came into force in April 1997, extends its effectiveness not only to the Defense structures, but to the broader national context of civil industry. In prohibiting the development, production, purchase, conservation, possession, transfer and use of chemical weapons and imposing their destruction and any production facilities, it also represents a non-proliferation treaty.
The Treaty assigns to all participants the right to carry out observation flights on the territories of any other member state and the duty to accept observation flights on their territories in accordance with the modalities established by the treaty itself.
Furthermore, in accordance with the provisions of the same Convention, Defense contributes, in collaboration with the MAE, to supporting demining and stockpile destruction activities in the countries most at risk. An Italian officer is present in the mission that the WEU, with EU funding, has arranged for Croatia (WEUDAM).
The destruction of the mines in storage is still underway and their complete elimination is expected by 2002.
The Open Skies Treaty, aimed at promoting greater openness and transparency through measures aimed at strengthening trust and security in military activities, provides for the overflight of the States Parties (from Vancouver to Vladivostok) with an aircraft equipped with sensors. Signed in 1992 by the CFE themselves (NATO and ex-Warsaw Pact), it will come into force on 1 January 2002.
The Ottawa Convention on the ban on anti-personnel mines, of December 1997, was in fact anticipated by Italy both with the decision of the Council of Ministers of 13 June 1997 (ban on production, export, storage and use) and with the subsequent law n. 374 of 29 October 1997.
In particular, the Defense will be able to provide its contribution of expertise, or in the context of Peace Operations, in confiscation/seizure activities, reduction and security of deposits.

2.1 The line of command
2.1.1 The Head of State The Constitution (art. 87), as regards Defence, attributes the following powers to the President of the Republic: the Command of the Armed Forces; • the presidency of the Supreme Defense Council; • the declaration of the state of war decided by the Chambers.
In particular, the provision of the aforementioned L. n. 25/1997 that govemment resolutions on security and defense must be examined by the Supreme Defense Council and approved by Parliament means that the President of the Republic exercises further control over the delicate matter.
Furthermore, this appears to be in accordance with the composition of the body in which the Ministers responsible for strategic sectors of the Nation participate.

2.1.3 The Parliament The Parliament is the constitutional body that directly represents the popular will. The Constitution (articles 56, 57,
70 and 71) gives Parliament a central position and role in the political-constitutional system, configuring it as the most important link between the community and the rulers. Its centrality in the constitutional structure takes place through the ownership of functions of pre-eminent importance, among which the fundamental ones are the legislative function and that of political direction and control of the Govemment. It seems clear that the two fundamental functions of Parliament are inserted in a decisive and incisive way into the general policy of the Govemment and therefore into that of state security. national community.
Furthermore, the involvement of Parliament in govemment deliberations on defense and security entails a more shared political responsibility in the adoption of decisive choices for the future of the Nation and also contributes to establishing a respectful collaborative relationship between military leaders and political leaders of everyone's skills.
At the request of the President of the Republic, other Ministers, in addition to those indicated, other institutional positions of the State and the Armed Forces, as well as subjects with particular expertise in the scientific, industrial and economic fields, may participate.

2.2 The restructuring of the Armed Forces
2.2.1 Legislative legislation The current restructuring of the Defense largely traces its origins back to November 1986, when the then Minister of Defense Giovanni Spadolini presented to the Senate a draft bill aimed at revising the structure in a unified sense at the top of the Dicastery with the attribution of very specific responsibilities at the political and military levels. In fact, a Chief of Defense Minister who was no longer "primus inter" was envisaged The initial trigger of the latest changes was promoted by law 28 December 1995, n. 549 "Measurements of Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it they support the discussion in accordance with the Minister's directives and respond to questions and interpellations.
In reality, the changed intemational scenario, the crisis situations that arise suddenly, the various hotbeds of tension that manifest themselves in areas of priority national strategic interest, but also in other geographical areas where Italy has found itself operating in support of peace and human rights, had already induced the various Defense Ministers who followed after the collapse of the Berlin Wall to propose subsequent restructuring of the Armed Forces in light of the criterion, also followed by other nations, of reducing their quantity in favor of quality. parliamentarians;
1. the Armed Forces are at the service of the Republic;
2. the organization and activity of the Armed Forces comply with articles 11 and 52 of the Constitution and with the law;
3. the priority task of the Armed Forces is the defense of the State;
4. the Armed Forces also have the task of operating to achieve peace and security, in compliance with the rules of intemational law and the decisions of the intemational organizations of which Italy is a member; 5. the Armed Forces contribute to the protection of free institutions and carry out specific tasks in circumstances of public disasters and in other cases of extraordinary necessity and urgency.

2.2.2 The succession of Legislative Decrees In particular, with the Legislative Decree of 16 July 1997, n. 264, the reorganization of the central area of the Ministry of Defense was set up, in order to simplify the difficult links between the General Directorates, the Central Offices and the General Staff. The Decree substantially halved the organizational structures of the central area of the Dicastery, with the reduction of the General Directorates from 19 to 10 and the Central Offices from 5 to 2.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it At the same time, Law no. was approved on 18 February 1997. 25 called "Reform of the Military Leaders".
Subsequently, the aforementioned Legislative Decrees were forwarded for consideration to the two houses of Parliament.
Therefore, this Decree is considered one of the main provisions of the reform, because it determines - after more than thirty years
- a drastic reduction of the technical-operational area of Defence. In fact, it: - defines the provisions for the suppression, revision and adaptation of the Commands/Bodies of the territorial, operational and training organization of each individual Armed Force and joint forces, reducing, among others, the number of Territorial Military Commands of the Armed Forces ( Military Region Commands for the Army, Chief Commands of the Maritime Military Department for the Navy, Air Region Commands for the Air Force); • seeks to pursue a better overall balance of the operational tool in its various components and articulations, attempting to achieve a qualitative increase in forces and an increase in extemal projection capacity, with accentuated "response" readiness, high autonomy and logistical sustainability, in coherence with the force structure defined in the new NATO doctrine;

2.2.8 Security The President of the Council of Ministers is assigned top management, general political responsibility and coordination of information and security policy in the interest and for the defense of the democratic State and the institutions established by the Constitution as its foundation (art. 1 law no. 801).
The Minister of Defence, on whom the SISMI depends, establishes its organization and takes care of its activities on the basis of the directives and provisions of the President of the Council of Ministers.
The primary and direct competence of the Minister of Defense in the sector of Military Policy is thus further reaffirmed and therefore in the issuing of the consequent guidelines to all dependent bodies. Just as he is assigned the tool for monitoring the achievement of the objectives set in the "ministerial directive". the Presidential Decree of 14 May 2001, n. 241 "Regulations for the organization of the offices of direct collaboration of the Minister of Defense" which, in defining the structure of the Cabinet, establishes in particular the establishment of the Office for Military Policy - included in the past in the Defense General Staff - and of the Service of intemal control, recently established in all ministries.
In this regard, it is necessary to underline that some States use their information services as an offensive tool of "indirect strategy" whose dynamics are not limited to military espionage but above all reveal forms of political, industrial, technological espionage or subversion, sabotage, disinformation , penetration into the Armed Forces and manipulation of social groups. The counter-espionage activity conducted by SISMI tends to prevent all forms of indirect strategy, applied against the Italian State and its systems, and in any case to prevent them from achieving their objectives. (officers, non-commissioned officers and enlisted men of the Army, Navy and Air Force);

An inter-ministerial Committee for Information and Security has been established at the Presidency of the Council of Ministers with consultancy and proposal functions for the President of the Council of Ministers, on the general guidelines and fundamental objectives to be pursued within the framework of the information and security policy. ____________ In essence, the SISMI represents a defense tool and its activity:
The SISMI carries out all information and security tasks for the military defense of the independence and integrity of the State from any danger, threat or aggression. The SISMI also carries out, for the aforementioned purposes, counter-espionage tasks (art. 4 law no. 801).
Every decision-making act must be supported by reliable information elements: the more in line with reality the decision is, the greater the availability of reliable information.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it is therefore confirmed that the assessment of the threat to Italy is institutionally entrusted to the Military Security Information Service (Law 24 October 1977 n. 801 "Institutions and regulations of services for information and security and regulation of state secrets" ).
The activity of the SISMI is carried out within the exclusive framework of the directives received from the political authorities and another important aspect of it is represented by the development of analyzes and situations.
2.2.8.1 The SISMI The entire restructuring process could not fail to also impact information and security activities, albeit to a lesser extent as the basic system, the one relating to the SISMI, has not undergone any changes.
The SISMI is required to communicate to the Minister of Defense and to the Executive Committee for Information and Security Services (CESIS) all the information received or in its possession, the analyzes and situations developed, the operations carried out and everything relating to its activity (art. 4 law n. 801).

In the area of responsibilities, Commiservizi pursues and achieves particular objectives, such as: transparency, through the strengthening of the URP and the website on which the contractual activity is advertised in terms of planning, execution and conclusion and the legislation which regulates the sector; spending control, through which it was possible to achieve price savings of around 20% compared to the past; search for maximum cost-effectiveness, through the reduction and merging of tenders, with the achievement of savings of over 57% compared to the past; timeliness of administrative action, with the achievement of the objective of settling invoices in 40-60 days, instead of the 90 required by current legislation; "pushed" informativeness; resource optimization; reduction of litigation; regulatory renewal.

2.3.4.3 The School and Training Organization (fig. 7) In the context of the reorganization of the Armed Forces, the reconfiguration of the school-training area has taken on particular importance, following both the pressing need to update the training and training cycles of the personnel, and the need to move away from the logic of an in-depth initial preparation with a few episodes of "updating", to move on to permanent training, congenial to the application of an increasingly complex and sophisticated training method.
52


• • The Training Inspector, in addition to the Inspectorate staff, employs the training bodies listed in the table below. The total personnel amounts to 2065 officers, 2136 non-commissioned officers, 9906 enlisted men and 815 civilians.
• the Army Logistics Inspectorate, responsible for all management activities, both at a central level, also through the resources of the pre-existing Logistics Corps Commands, later incorporated into Inspectorate Departments, and on the territory; the Land Operational Forces Command.
First of all, the Inspectorate for Training and Specialization (Rome) was recently established, responsible for the basic training, specialization and subsequent qualification of all Armed Forces personnel. It was established by merging the preexisting Weapons and Schools Inspectorates and directs the activity of all the Schools for Officers, Non-Commissioned Officers and Volunteers of the Army. the EMS Logistics Department, essentially responsible for policy functions, i.e. the preparation of financial planning proposals for modemization and renovation, as well as the subsequent implementation of the programs by defining the technical-operational requirements of the new materials, development, testing, technical-operational evaluation and assignment of mandates to the General Directorates responsible for procurement;
To achieve these results, both the training sector of the actual permanent service categories, and the school-training sector responsible for the qualification/specialization of all personnel of all ranks of the Army, were affected by changes.
The structure of ISPEL (fig. 8) includes: • the Logistics Inspector and his office; • a Coordination and General Support Department; • four Departments, divided into homogeneous functional areas.
In this context, it was decided to redefine the top of the Command and Control chain of the logistics organization, which sees three fundamental actors reporting to the Head of SME, each of whom has very distinct tasks and responsibilities:
Among the functional sectors of the Armed Force that have been revised the most, the logistics organization must be mentioned, which has undergone a profound transformation, from a static organization, largely territorial and aimed at well-defined employment hypotheses, to a streamlined organization, with a integrated, efficient and well-defined Command and Control chain, ready to support the multiple forms of use that can be attributed to the Armed Force today. ____________

2.3.4.5 The Territorial Organization The restructuring of the Territorial Organization has made it possible to eliminate the areas of overlap/duplication of competence deriving from the indeterminacy, in some sectors, of the three lines of command "operational", "logistics" and "territorial " and to achieve a reduction in the Command and logistical-administrative support structures.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it The basis of the organization's functioning is the desire to implement logistical support that is no longer "just in case", which presupposes preparing or carrying out everything that could possibly be needed, with a great waste of resources and energy, but rather "just in time" logistical support, such as to provide only what is needed and at the desired time, making use of the most modem management systems for all sectors and for all activities, from supply to maintenance; this, with a notable saving of resources previously immobilized in the bodies present at the various links of the logistics chain.

The "Directive" should have been updated every two years, i.e. in January 2001.
Second mission Safeguarding the Euro-Atlantic spaces, within the framework of the strategic and/or vital interests of the country, through the contribution to the collective defense of NATO.
This mission finds its foundation in the agreements signed and ratified by Parliament relating to NATO and in the statements of the new Strategic Concept of the Atlantic Alliance, signed in Washington in 1999. mass (WMD);
In summary, the "1999 Ministerial Directive" was supposed to constitute the first national attempt to set up the subsequent planning activities of the Armed Forces on the authoritative basis of upstream directives issued by the Minister.
It derives directly from the constitutional provisions and was developed through the reunification of the pre-existing Armed Forces missions relating to the defense of the national territory. It should be highlighted that the reference to the integrity of the national territory and the security and freedom of the communication routes does not limit the action to defend the vital interests of the country to the classic forms of threat only, but can be extended to the most recent forms of asymmetric conflict such as defense against terrorist attacks or the use of weapons of mass destruction.
• increase in interoperability and cooperation capabilities between NATO forces and partner countries; • maintaining adequate levels of the military budget.
• the integrity of the national territory understood as a land platform, airspace and territorial waters; • the security and freedom of communication routes; • the security of areas of national sovereignty and of compatriots abroad.
• projectability of forces (strategic transport, autonomy and logistical sustainability); • active and passive measures and deterrence capabilities against the risk and threat of weapons of destruction Defense of the country's vital interests against any possible aggression, in order to safeguard: ____________
2.4.2 The Missions These are, therefore, missions which, without prejudice to the tasks of the Armed Forces established by Law 331/2000, may be susceptible to variations depending on the changed global strategic scenario.
First mission Fourth mission Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it Therefore the "ministerial directive" and the "strategic concept", issued in times following the restructuring produced by the various Legislative Decrees, could not constitute the logical prerequisite for the formation of the new Italian defensive instrument. They were therefore translated into indications for the use of the tool and as ideas for improvements to be made to the structure during construction. Hence the definition of "missions", to be understood as the translation of the legislative norms into the national and intemational reference framework and therefore implementing the directives of the national security policy.
The third mission arises from a specific political will, constantly confirmed over the last twenty years and formalised, in fact, with the 1999 Ministerial Directive which led the Armed Forces to participate in prevention and management actions of crises decided by the UN , or in any case inspired by the principles enunciated by the United Nations Charter, according to three possible methods of intervention: • in the role of "Lead Nation" (for example, Operation "ALBA"); • as a Force forming part of a Coalition (such as NATO, the EU and the WEU); • following bi-multilateral agreements (Technical-Military Mission in Malta).
From the "directive" an implementation document, called "strategic concept", was to be issued by the Chief of Defense Staff, which was issued on 28 December 2000.
Third mission Management of intemational crises. Participation in crisis prevention and management operations, in order to guarantee peace, security, stability and intemational legality, as well as the affirmation of fundamental human rights, in the spirit of the United Nations Charter, within the framework of intemational organizations and/or bi-multilateral agreements, with particular regard to the autonomous European crisis management capacity.

In order to proceed with the technical-financial processing, these expenses are further divided into expenses bound "to laws" and bound "to programs".
- personnel, relating to the costs to be bome for the salaries and allowances of military personnel in permanent service, of civilian personnel with continuous employment relationships, for the economic treatment of conscript personnel as well as for the temporary retirement treatment of military personnel; - exercise, relating to the costs necessary to guarantee the functionality and efficiency of the military instrument (expenses for education and training, for the maintenance and support of vehicles, materials and infrastructures, for the functioning of the Commands/Bodies/Departments) ; - investment, relating to the costs necessary to improve the effectiveness of the military instrument (expenses for the modemization and renewal of operational means, technical-logistic support and infrastructure and for research and development).
The first two sectors (personnel and operations) determine the costs relating to the "functioning" of the Armed Forces, while the last concems the acquisition of durable goods, which in tum produce security and technological progress.
Expenses bound "to laws" are those that are mandatory in that they are determined, also in their extent, by legal provisions and/or by govemment resolutions. These charges are subject to automatic mechanisms that make them particularly rigid precisely because they are linked to laws and/or inter-ministerial or intemational agreements. They may also include particular needs oriented towards public utility services such as, for example, the water supply of the smaller islands, activities in favor of civil aviation, state civil air transport and for the rescue of seriously ill and traumatized patients.
3.2 Budget preparation Spending forecasts are drawn up on the basis of the objectives and indications provided by: - Govemment, with the Economic and Financial Planning Document (DPEF); - Minister of Economy and Finance, with the annual Circular relating to budget forecasts; - Minister of Defence, in relation to the financial hypothesis to be used as a reference (percentage ratio to be ensured between Defense Budget/GDP and Defense Function/GDP) and with the Investment Planning Document; - Chief of Defense Staff, with the "Directive for the preparation of the expenditure forecast".
3.1 ARTICULATION OF DEFENSE COSTS The expenses linked to "programmes" are those intended to ensure the functionality, efficiency and effectiveness of the military instrument and derive from the programmatic choices identified by the General Staff, the General Secretariat of Defense and the General Command of the Carabinieri ( called Top Planning Bodies of the Armed Forces and Joint Forces - OP) and approved by the Chief of Defense Staff.

3.1 Breakdown of Defense expenses The financial resources assigned to Defense are divided based on their destination into: - expenses for the Defense Function relating to the fulfillment of the purely institutional tasks devolved to the three Armed Forces (Army, Navy and Air Force); - expenses for the Public Security Function allocated to meet the needs of the Carabinieri; - expenses for Extemal Functions devolved to satisfy particular needs, regulated by Laws or Decrees, which are not directly connected with the institutional tasks of Defence; - expenses for provisional pensions relating to the payment of temporary retirement benefits to military personnel in the auxiliary position (period following termination from active service during which the soldier may be retained or recalled to service).

Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it In fact, when a new weapon system comes online, the need for the construction and/or adaptation of infrastructures, the start of training programs and specific exercises, as well as the maintenance of numbers and efficiency is connected and consequential. of the system itself, in addition to the numerous other activities that indirectly contribute to general support or which in any case constitute costs that are passed on to the individual company.
It should be underlined, however, that the costs are affected, to a considerable extent, by any changes that have occurred both in the architecture of the FA and in the structure of the individual component, and in relation to the tasks and functions developed by them. Furthermore, a further element of impact on their variability is represented by "military inflation" - very different from the more well-known national inflation - which must be resorted to in consideration of the particular characteristics of military spending which, due to its atypical nature, is difficult to lends itself to the use of classic indicators at national level (Gross Domestic Product, public administration indices, etc.). ____________ In this context it is considered significant to highlight that the resources that the Defense allocates to the exercise of the military instrument should not be considered pure "management expenses" but, in a much stricter sense, real tools of functionality and efficiency. And all this in particular in the current delicate phase of transition towards the professional model where the quality of the men's training is accompanied by the availability of an old fleet of materials, vehicles and structures, requiring continuous and increasingly costly maintenance and repairs.
The costs of the various military components of each FA (for example the brigade, the ship, the wing) arise from statistical calculations based on information acquired through automated detection systems which have, depending on the component in question, as the object of investigation: - its effective strength; - training activities (exercises and courses) and operations conducted on national territory in the year under review; - the infrastructures used and the consumption connected to their use; - the supplies of vehicles and materials and related consumption.

3.4 Costs The FF.AA. they constitute a tool of very high technological content and, consequently, of high cost due to the inherent need to maintain the competitive advantage at the highest levels.
These systems constitute a very valid tool both for carrying out the necessary checks in terms of cost/effectiveness
- in the financial planning/programming phase, as in the organisational-executive phase of the annual budget - and for estimating: - any costs to be incurred for the “ex-novo” creation of a different type of component (plant costs); - the possible savings obtainable in the event of dissolution or change in the type of a component; - the levels of efficiency and effectiveness of the component, through comparisons between the standard reference costs and those actually incurred.
This has an initial impact, obviously, on the resources that must be constantly and adequately allocated to Investment and Development. But immediately after the realization of each of the planned projects, the FF.AA. they must absorb operating costs which, precisely because of the high technological content, are also generally very considerable.
Standard costs represent the "optimal" costs that each structure should approach to guarantee the maximum cost/ effectiveness ratio. They are processed taking into account the financial data relating to seven types or macro-aggregates: fixed costs (also including salaries, wages and expenses for food, clothing and hygiene), infrastructure, training, healthcare, efficiency maintenance, number maintenance and residual .

4.2.1 the General Directorate of Land Armaments (TERRARM), the General Directorate of Naval Armaments (NAVARM) and the General Directorate of Aeronautical Armaments (ARMAEREO) responsible for the study, research and technical development, construction, procurement,
4.1 Tasks-Organization The organization of Defense as regards the industrial apparatus, is headed by the administrative area at the top of which is the Secretary General/DNA on whom the 10 General Directorates of the Ministry depend.
The Deputy General Secretary (civil) is a General Manager to whom the SG/DNA has delegated matters of an administrative nature which concem affairs of a general, personal, accident prevention, litigation, benefits, expense control, standardization of contractual procedures. In this capacity he deals with the General Directorates responsible for personnel, recruitment and services.
The aforementioned law on the reform of the Top Management provides that the position of Secretary General can be held by an officer of the Army, Navy or Air Force with the rank of lieutenant general, or corresponding, in permanent service or by a civilian of the Administration public, or extemal to it, in relation to specific professional experiences and qualifications.
In his role as DNA (National Director of Armaments), the Secretary General is responsible for directing the research, development and acquisition of weapons systems, based on the programs defined by the Chief of Defense Staff. In this context, it participates in high intemational fora in the framework of the implementation of multinational agreements.
The staff of the General Secretariat of Defense and National Armaments Directorate, having also absorbed some functions of the dissolved General Directorates and Central Offices and having to implement the reorganization resulting from law 25/97 and the related regulation referred to in Presidential Decree 556/2000 ( modified and integrated precisely as regards the General Secretariat by the recent Presidential Decree 172/2000), will amount to approximately 490 units, compared to the 629 pre-existing at the end of '97.
In the exercise of his duties, the General Secretary/DNA makes use of a "Staff" made up of The SG/DNA is a member of the Superior Council of the Armed Forces, of the Committee of Chiefs of Staff and is President of the Advisory Committee which examines the most important contracts in the field of armaments. He is also Vice President of the Defense - Industry Committee, chaired by the Minister of Defence.
Publications Center for Peace Studies www.studiperlapace.it General Secretariat and National Armaments Directorate, within which two Deputy General Secretaries operate, one military and the other civilian, who at present, on the basis of the delegated powers, are responsible for two different areas of interest divided into 6 departments indicated in the graphic.
This staff will have to enable it to carry out both "intemal" and intemational functions, the latter of which is continually increasing and which in other countries are managed by structures made up of at least 800/1000 people.

Furthermore, he deals with the General Directorates responsible for the acquisition of weapons systems.
